Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Whenever we’re supposed to sit and do quiet activities here, I just can’t seem to keep myself still and focused on them. Since moving into my senior living complex, I've really struggled to stay put and pay attention during those structured times.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have trouble with impulsive behavior, like interrupting others, acting without thinking, or feeling unable to wait your turn, or is it mainly difficulty sitting still and paying attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely struggle with both of those things. During our resident meetings, I often blurt out answers and jump in before people can even finish their sentences. And waiting my turn in lines, like for lunch or blood pressure checks, makes me so fidgety that I have to actively stop myself from slipping ahead.\n\nDoctor: Have these attention, restlessness, and impulsive behaviors been present since childhood or early adulthood, or did they start only later in life?\n\nPatient: Oh, they've actually been with me my whole life, ever since I was a little girl. Even back in grade school, my teachers were constantly telling me to stay in my seat and stop blurting out answers. My mother always used to say I was \"on the go\" from the very moment I woke up.\n\nDoctor: Were you also very hyperactive and impulsive as a child, or was it mostly trouble paying attention and staying organized?\n\nPatient: I was definitely much more hyperactive and impulsive rather than having trouble with organizing things. Even back in grade school, the teachers were always telling me to stay in my seat and stop blurting out answers, and my mother said I was \"on the go\" from the moment I woke up.\n\nDoctor: Did your childhood symptoms include significant trouble paying attention and getting organized, or was it mostly hyperactivity and impulsive behavior?\n\nPatient: It was almost entirely the hyperactivity and impulsivity, rather than trouble with paying attention or organizing. Back in school, my teachers were constantly telling me to stay in my seat and stop blurting out answers, and my mother always said I was \"on the go\" from the moment I woke up.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often get into serious trouble for breaking rules, fighting, bullying, lying, stealing, or damaging things?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I don't recall ever getting into that kind of trouble as a child. I was never aggressive or destructive, just very restless and talkative.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing trouble with paying attention, following through on tasks, or making careless mistakes, or has it mostly been hyperactivity and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It has almost entirely been the hyperactivity and impulsivity, rather than trouble with paying attention or making careless mistakes. My main struggle has always been that constant need to be on the go and finding it so incredibly hard to just sit quietly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you get very angry, lose your temper, or deliberately argue with or defy authority figures, even when you know it will cause problems?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those kinds of episodes at all. I don't lose my temper or deliberately defy or argue with the staff here. My difficulties are just about finding it hard to sit still, wait my turn, or keep quiet, which comes from my restlessness rather than any anger or defiance.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with paying attention, following through on tasks, or making careless mistakes, or is it mostly restlessness and impulsive behavior?"

I don't have trouble completing tasks or being careless; I just can't seem to keep myself still.

My main struggle has always been that constant need to be on the go and finding it so incredibly hard to just sit quietly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you get very angry, lose your temper, or deliberately argue with or defy authority figures, even when you know it will cause problems?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those kinds of episodes at all. My difficulties are just about finding it hard to sit still, wait my turn, or keep quiet, which comes from my restlessness rather than any anger or defiance.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with paying attention, following through on tasks, or making careless mistakes, or is it mostly restlessness and impulsive behavior?\n\nPatient: It really is almost entirely the restlessness and impulsive behavior rather than trouble with inattention or making careless mistakes. Back in school, my teachers were constantly telling me to stay in my seat and stop blurting out answers, and my mother always said I was \"on the go\" from the moment I woke up.\n\nDoctor: As a child, did you often get into serious trouble for breaking rules, fighting, bullying, lying, stealing, or damaging things?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I don't recall ever getting into that kind of trouble as a child. I was never aggressive or destructive, just very restless and talkative.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing trouble with paying attention, following through on tasks, or making careless mistakes, or has it mostly been hyperactivity and impulsivity?\n\nPatient: It has almost entirely been the hyperactivity and impulsivity, rather than trouble with paying attention or making careless mistakes. My main struggle has always been that constant need to be on the go and finding it so incredibly hard to just sit quietly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you get very angry, lose your temper, or deliberately argue with or defy authority figures, even when you know it will cause problems?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience those kinds of episodes at all. I don't lose my temper or deliberately defy or argue with the staff here. My difficulties are just about finding it hard to sit still, wait my turn, or keep quiet, which comes from my restlessness rather than any anger or defiance.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have ongoing problems with paying attention, following through on tasks, or making careless mistakes, or is it mostly restlessness and impulsive behavior?\n\nPatient: It really is almost entirely the restlessness and impulsive behavior rather than trouble with inattention or making careless mistakes. I've always been \"on the go\" and struggle with a physical need to move, fidgeting, and impatience, like blurting out answers or hating to wait in lines. I don't have trouble completing tasks or being careless; I just can't seem to keep myself still.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors happen in more than one setting, such as at school and at home, and did they cause problems there?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely happened in both places before I was twelve. At school, my teachers were constantly telling me to stay in my seat and stop blurting out answers, and at home, my mother always said I was \"on the go\" from the moment I woke up.\n\nDoctor: Did these lifelong restlessness and impulsive behaviors cause clear problems in your daily life, such as at school, at home, or in relationships, and if so, how did they affect you?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely caused issues; back in grade school I was constantly told to stay in my seat, and my mother always said I was \"on the go.\" Now, in my senior complex, I get gentle scoldings and reminders from staff and residents for interrupting, pacing, and not waiting my turn. It's become so embarrassing that I've actually started avoiding some of the group activities altogether.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors continue into your teens and adulthood without long breaks, or were there long periods when they went away?\n\nPatient: They never really went away; it has been a continuous pattern throughout my entire life. During my younger and adult years, I managed my restlessness by keeping myself constantly busy with my garden, art projects, and volunteering. It has only become a real problem again now because of the highly structured environment here at the senior complex."

I don't have trouble completing tasks or being careless; I just can't seem to keep myself still.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors happen in more than one setting, such as at school and at home, and did they cause problems there?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely happened in both places before I was twelve. At school, my teachers were constantly telling me to stay in my seat and stop blurting out answers, and at home, my mother always said I was \"on the go\" from the moment I woke up.\n\nDoctor: Did these lifelong restlessness and impulsive behaviors cause clear problems in your daily life, such as at school, at home, or in relationships, and if so, how did they affect you?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely caused issues; back in grade school I was constantly told to stay in my seat, and my mother always said I was \"on the go.\" Now, in my senior complex, I get gentle scoldings and reminders from staff and residents for interrupting, pacing, and not waiting my turn. It's become so embarrassing that I've actually started avoiding some of the group activities altogether.\n\nDoctor: Did these restlessness and impulsive behaviors continue into your teens and adulthood without long breaks, or were there long periods when they went away?\n\nPatient: They never really went away; it has been a continuous pattern throughout my entire life. During my younger and adult years, I managed my restlessness by keeping myself constantly busy with my garden, art projects, and volunteering. It has only become a real problem again now because of the highly structured environment here at the senior complex.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any long periods in your life when these restlessness and impulsive behaviors completely went away, or have they been present continuously since childhood?\n\nPatient: They have been present continuously since childhood, starting when my grade school teachers had to constantly tell me to stay in my seat. They never truly went away, but they were much easier to manage when I lived independently and could channel my energy into my garden, art, and volunteering. It is only since moving into this highly structured senior complex that they have become a noticeable problem again."

Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
